## Management Summary

### Verdict

🔴 Juice Shop is not production-ready. Unauthenticated attackers can reach admin functions and extract the full customer database using publicly committed credentials, while persistent scripts in user-submitted content enable mass session theft.

**Risk distribution:** 🔴 Critical: 8 · 🟠 High: 24 · 🟡 Medium: 20 · 🟢 Low: n/a · **Total: 52**<br/>**Reporting threshold:** medium - Low and Informational excluded<br/>**Assessment evidence:** 48 confirmed-exploitable finding(s) · 7 implementation weakness(es) · 7 design weakness(es)



**Basis:** a code-derived threat model at implementation level - built from repository evidence, not a planning document. Design intent, business processes, runtime behaviour and production-only configuration are outside what this analysis can see (see [§11 Out of Scope](#11-out-of-scope)).

<br/>

**What an attacker can do today, worst first:**

<blockquote style="border-left: 3px solid #dc2626; background: #fef2f2; padding: 16px 20px; margin: 0;">

- **Admin access without a `password`** — Any visitor forges a valid admin login token using the server key committed to the public repository. *(🔴 [F-001](#f-001) — JWT Verification Without Algorithm Allowlist, 🔴 [F-004](#f-004) — Hardcoded RSA signing key in source (`lib/insecurity.ts:21`) → [W-001](#w-001), [W-004](#w-004))* — ✓ verified attack path
- **Full database extracted without login** — Unauthenticated attackers read every `user` account and `password` `hash` via the login form's injectable query. *(🔴 [F-007](#f-007) — SQL Injection → [W-002](#w-002))*
- **Active sessions stolen across all customers** — Stored scripts in product descriptions and `user` content execute in every visitor's browser, capturing login tokens. *(🔴 [F-009](#f-009) — Cross-Site Scripting (XSS))*
- **Any account reachable via a known `password`** — Seeded admin and demo accounts use fixed passwords that ship with every deployment. *(🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — Hard-coded seeded account credentials (`data/datacreator.ts:196`), 🔴 [F-006](#f-006) — Predictable derived password (`frontend/src/app/oauth/oauth.component.ts:30`) → [W-004](#w-004))*
- **Any customer's orders exposed to peers** — Logged-in users access other customers' orders and addresses by incrementing numeric record identifiers, with no ownership check. *(🔴 [F-008](#f-008) — Insecure Direct Object Reference → [W-003](#w-003))*
- **Self-granted admin `role` for any `user`** — Any authenticated `user` promotes their own account to admin by including a `role` field in a profile update request. *(🔴 [F-010](#f-010) — Mass assignment privileged field accepted from request body `routes/verify.ts:53`)* — ✓ verified attack path

</blockquote>

<br/>

Remediation requires fixing the token verifier, rotating all committed keys and credentials, parameterizing database queries, and enabling server-side authorization checks.

### Top Weaknesses

The systemic root-cause problems behind the findings - the classes to fix, not just individual bugs. Each links to the full [Weakness Register](#7-weakness-register) with its findings and remediation.

- 🔴 **[W-001](#w-001) - Authentication relies on hand-rolled token verification** (Critical) - Token validation is implemented in application code rather than behind one vetted and consistently configured authentication path. _Proven by [F-001](#f-001), [F-015](#f-015), [F-044](#f-044) (+2 more)._
- 🔴 **[W-002](#w-002) - Database access relies on concatenated queries** (Critical) - Database queries are assembled from application values instead of passing those values through an enforced parameterised data-access path. _Proven by [F-007](#f-007)._
- 🔴 **[W-003](#w-003) - `Authorization` is implemented route by route** (Critical) - `Authorization` depends on per-handler checks instead of a policy boundary that consistently enforces `role`, ownership, and tenant scope. _Proven by 🔴 [F-002](#f-002), 🔴 [F-008](#f-008), 🔴 [F-035](#f-035) (+2 more)._
- 🔴 **[W-004](#w-004) - Secrets are committed to source instead of a managed store** (Critical) - Cryptographic keys, credentials, and other high-entropy secrets are embedded as literals in source or `config` rather than resolved at runtime from a managed secret store, so anyone with repository r… _Proven by 🔴 [F-004](#f-004), 🔴 [F-005](#f-005), 🔴 [F-031](#f-031) (+1 more)._
- 🟠 **[W-005](#w-005) - Endpoints are reachable without enforced authentication** (High) - Sensitive API routes and real-time channels are exposed without an enforced authentication check at the endpoint boundary. _Proven by [F-013](#f-013)._
- 🟠 **[W-006](#w-006) - Denial of Service is implemented inconsistently** (High) - Resource-consuming operations lack rate limiting or bounds, so a single actor can exhaust capacity. _Proven by [F-033](#f-033), [F-064](#f-064), [F-065](#f-065) (+3 more)._

Architecture tiers top-to-bottom (External Actors → Client → Application → Data) with the top threats per component. The in-figure legend on the right explains the attack scenarios, severity dots and symbols. ![Figure 1 - Architecture & Top Threats](threat-model-juice-shop-thorough-v0.6.0b2.figure1.svg)

**Figure 2 - Risk Flow: Actor → Tier → Impact**

Heatmap: **actors** (left) → **architecture tiers** (middle, Client → Application → Data) → **impact** (right). Numbered red arrows ①–⑤ are the threats enumerated in the Top Threats table below.

![Figure 2 - Risk Flow: Actor to Tier to Impact](threat-model-juice-shop-thorough-v0.6.0b2.figure2.svg)

**Threat actors.** The actors below drive the numbered attack paths in the figures above. The **Shop User** is the *victim* of client-side attacks (XSS / CSRF), not an attacker - in Figure 2 the compromise surfaces as the resulting business-impact node rather than as a separate actor box.

- **Shop User** — legitimate customer; target of client-side attacks; target of ⑤ Output Encoding / Cross-Site Scripting.
- **Anonymous Internet Attacker** — no account; registers in seconds when needed; drives ① Hardcoded Secrets & Weak Cryptography, ② Insecure Query Construction & Data Access, ④ Sensitive File & Secret Exposure.
- **Authenticated Internet Attacker** — owns a regular account; logged in; drives ③ Broken `Authorization` & Access Control.

**Verified attack chains.** 3 fully viable ([AC-T-003](#ac-t-003), [AC-T-004](#ac-t-004), [AC-T-005](#ac-t-005)); 2 partially blocked ([AC-T-001](#ac-t-001), [AC-T-002](#ac-t-002)). These chains combine individual findings into end-to-end exploitation paths verified step-by-step against the code - see [§9 Abuse Cases](#9-abuse-cases) for the per-step breakdown and blocking mitigations.

| **3** | [C-02](#c-02) — Express\.js API Server | ● [M-001](#m-001) — Enforce JWT signature and algorithm verification (`insecurity.ts:189`) | 🔴 [F-001](#f-001) — JWT Verification Without Algorithm Allowlist (`lib/insecurity.ts`) | Low |
| **4** | [C-02](#c-02) — Express\.js API Server | ● [M-007](#m-007) — Use parameterized database queries (`login.ts:34`) | 🔴 [F-007](#f-007) — SQL Injection (`routes/login.ts`) | Low |
| **5** | [C-02](#c-02) — Express\.js API Server | ● [M-004](#m-004) — Move cryptographic keys to a managed secret store (`insecurity.ts:21`) | 🔴 [F-004](#f-004) — Hardcoded RSA signing key in source (`lib/insecurity.ts`) | Medium |
| **6** | [C-02](#c-02) — Express\.js API Server | ● [M-008](#m-008) — Enforce object-level (ownership) authorization (`wallet.ts:27`) | 🔴 [F-008](#f-008) — Insecure Direct Object Reference (`routes/wallet.ts`) | Medium |
| **7** | [C-02](#c-02) — Express\.js API Server | ● [M-010](#m-010) — Allowlist client-controlled fields (`verify.ts:53`) | 🔴 [F-010](#f-010) — Mass assignment privileged field accepted from request body (`routes/verify.ts`) | Medium |
| **8** | [C-04](#c-04) — SQLite Database | ● [M-005](#m-005) — Move secrets to a managed secret store (`datacreator.ts:196`) | 🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — Hard-coded seeded account credentials (`data/datacreator.ts`) | Medium |
| **9** | [C-02](#c-02) — Express\.js API Server | ◕ [M-002](#m-002) — Enforce server-side authorization on every endpoint (`server.ts:309`) | 🔴 [F-002](#f-002) — Missing Authentication on State-Changing Routes (`server.ts`) | Low |
| **10** | [C-02](#c-02) — Express\.js API Server | ◕ [M-036](#m-036) — Enforce correct server-side authorization (`deluxe.ts:43`) | 🔴 [F-036](#f-036) — Deluxe role granted without payment (`routes/deluxe.ts`) | Low |
| **11** | [C-02](#c-02) — Express\.js API Server | ◕ [M-035](#m-035) — Enforce server-side authorization on every endpoint (`chat.ts:184`) | 🔴 [F-035](#f-035) — Coupon discount unbounded server-side (`routes/chat.ts`) | Medium |
| **12** | [C-04](#c-04) — SQLite Database | ◕ [M-022](#m-022) — Encode output instead of bypassing the framework sanitizer (`user.ts:49`) | 🔴 [F-022](#f-022) — Legacy sanitizer on persisted username (`models/user.ts`) | Low |
| **13** | [C-05](#c-05) — GitHub Actions CI/CD Pipeline | ◕ [M-039](#m-039) — Enforce server-side authorization on every endpoint (`rebase.yml:10`) | 🔴 [F-039](#f-039) — Comment command without author authorization (`.github/workflows/rebase.yml`) | Low |
| **14** | [C-07](#c-07) — Web3 / Wallet / NFT Surface | ◕ [M-031](#m-031) — Move cryptographic keys to a managed secret store (`checkKeys.ts:10`) | 🔴 [F-031](#f-031) — Hardcoded wallet mnemonic in source (`routes/checkKeys.ts`) | Medium |

*25 additional P1/P2 mitigations capped from the leader-board · 29 P3 backlog items in [§10 Mitigation Register](#10-mitigation-register). Sorted by priority (P1 first), then component, then leverage (most findings first), severity (Critical first), and effort (Low first)`.*`

### AI / LLM Exposure

The Express\.js chat route embeds an LLM with tool-calling capability; the attack surface spans prompt injection, unconstrained tool execution, and system-prompt leakage. See **[§6 Security Architecture](#6-security-architecture)** for the per-control detail.



- **[LLM01](https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm01/) / [ASI01](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/) Prompt Injection** — User chat messages reach the language model `at ``routes/chat.ts:191` without sanitization or intent separation; crafted input can redirect the model to invoke tools outside the intended scope, including the coupon-issuance tool. _([C-02](#c-02) — Express\.js API Server)_
  - ↳ 🟠 [F-016](#f-016) — Prompt injection via unvalidated chat messages (`chat.ts:191`)

- **[LLM06](https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm06/) / [ASI02](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/) Excessive Agency** — The chatbot can issue coupon codes via a server-side tool call `at ``routes/chat.ts:184` without any approval gate or per-user rate limit; a malicious or injected prompt triggers unlimited discount issuance. _([C-02](#c-02) — Express\.js API Server)_
  - ↳ 🔴 [F-035](#f-035) — Coupon discount unbounded via chat tool (`chat.ts:184`)

- **[LLM07](https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm07/) System Prompt Leakage** — The confidential business-policy `system` prompt `at ``routes/chat.ts:105` is not protected against adversarial extraction; successful leakage exposes internal pricing rules and operations `data` to any chat `user`. _([C-02](#c-02) — Express\.js API Server)_
  - ↳ 🟡 [F-048](#f-048) — Confidential policy in LLM system prompt (`chat.ts:105`)

- **[LLM05](https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm05/) / [ASI05](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/) Improper Output Handling** — Tool-call arguments and intermediate model outputs are streamed to all connected WebSocket callers `at ``routes/chat.ts:228` without tenant isolation, leaking internal tool invocation `data` across concurrent users. _([C-02](#c-02) — Express\.js API Server, [C-06](#c-06) — Real-time WebSocket Channel)_
  - ↳ 🟡 [F-049](#f-049) — Tool-call arguments streamed to all callers (`chat.ts:228`)

- **[LLM10](https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm10/) Unbounded Consumption** — No rate limit or token cap is applied to the LLM backend `at ``server.ts:638`; any unauthenticated `user` can send continuous chat messages, driving unbounded inference costs. _([C-02](#c-02) — Express\.js API Server)_
  - ↳ 🟠 [F-033](#f-033) — Unbounded LLM token consumption (`server.ts:638`)

### Operational Strengths

Operational controls rated Adequate or Partial - grouped into broad clusters (full per-control breakdown in [§6](#6-security-architecture)). Clusters demoted to Weak by open Critical/High findings appear in [§6](#6-security-architecture) instead, not here.

<tbody>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>Hardened HTTP Stack</strong></td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><em>Browser-facing HTTP hardening - security headers, cookie flags, cross-origin policy, and abuse-protection limits.</em><br/>CORS Policy</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">⚠️ Partial - Bypassed by 1 High finding(s) of the kind this cluster is supposed to prevent - e.g.<br/>🟠 <a href="#f-023">F-023</a> — SSRF via profile image URL (<code>routes/profileImageUrlUpload.ts:24</code>).</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>Observability &amp; Audit</strong></td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><em>Runtime visibility - access logging, audit trails, and operational telemetry for post-incident review.</em><br/>Security Event Logging</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">⚠️ Partial - Coverage incomplete - see <a href="#7-weakness-register">§7</a> control assessment.</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>Container &amp; Supply-Chain Hardening</strong></td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><em>Build-time and runtime hardening - minimal base image, non-root execution, dependency inventory.</em><br/>Third-Party CI/CD Action Pinning</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">⚠️ Partial - Coverage incomplete - see <a href="#7-weakness-register">§7</a> control assessment.</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>Cryptographic Hygiene</strong></td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><em>Transport security, key lifecycle, and password-hash strength.</em><br/>TLS Transport Encryption</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">⚠️ Partial - Bypassed by 3 High finding(s) of the kind this cluster is supposed to prevent - e.g.<br/>🟠 <a href="#f-025">F-025</a> — MD5 password hashing (<code>lib/insecurity.ts:41</code>)<br/>🟠 <a href="#f-029">F-029</a> — Unsalted fast password hash (<code>models/user.ts:76</code>)<br/>🟠 <a href="#f-018">F-018</a> — Non-cryptographic RNG for a secret/token <code>lib/insecurity.ts:53</code>.</td></tr>
</tbody>
</table>


**Bottom line:** These controls narrow specific attack surfaces but `none` eliminates a Critical finding on its own.

## 1. System Overview

**Repository:** `https://github.com/juice-shop/juice-shop.git`

### Scope

This threat model covers 7 components of juice-shop2: **Angular SPA**, **Express\.js API Server**, **Authentication and Session Service**, **SQLite Database**, **GitHub Actions CI/CD Pipeline**, **Real-time WebSocket Channel**, **Web3 / Wallet / NFT Surface**.

All 7 modeled components received full STRIDE threat analysis.

**Out of scope:** third-party hosted dependencies, browser runtime, operating-system kernel, and the underlying network infrastructure.

**Basis:** a code-derived threat model at implementation level, built from source, configuration and git history. It describes the `system` as built, not as designed.

---

### Trust Boundaries

Canonical boundary crossings. **Assumption & verdict** names the condition that makes each crossing safe and whether this report still supports it, judged one condition at a time - which conditions apply follows from the direction of the crossing.

<tr><td style="white-space:nowrap"><a id="tb-3"></a>tb-3</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>api-server → external</strong><br/>Control: none identified</td><td>◐ <strong>Unverified</strong></td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">network · operator</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Nothing attacker-controlled from the user prompt reaches the LLM provider without sanitization or filtering.<br/><em>inferred</em><br/><em>No finding contradicts it.</em></td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">-</td></tr>

<tr><td style="white-space:nowrap"><a id="tb-6"></a>tb-6</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>auth-service → sqlite-database</strong><br/>Control: Sequelize user record lookup</td><td>🔒 <strong>Internal</strong></td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">in-process - enforcement interface, no trust transition</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Every user record lookup during authentication reaches SQLite through Sequelize ORM parameterized queries.<br/><em>confirmed</em><br/><strong>In doubt</strong> - 8 finding(s) in the components it covers, none linked here.</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">-</td></tr>
</tbody>
</table>

_Exposure, most exposed first: ⚠ Review (endpoints unresolved) · 🌐 External (reached from outside the model) · ◐ Unverified (crossing not confirmed) · 🔒 Internal (both ends modelled) · ↗ Egress (reaches outside the model)._

_Kind - the crossing mechanism (build pipeline, in-process, network), then after `·` what changes across it: data-origin, identity, operator, privilege, tenant. Shown alone, nothing changes._

_Conditions - **inbound**: validation, authentication, authorization · **outbound**: egress content, egress destination, response trust (replies are untrusted input) · **internal**: query construction (`data` never becomes program text), authentication, authorization._

_Verdict per condition - **broken**: a linked finding proves the gap · in doubt: related findings, `none` linked here · not examined: nothing bears on it. `N related` counts further findings on the same condition. Linked findings sit under the condition they break, or under _Unattributed_. A link raises effective severity only at a confirmed internet ingress; raw risk never changes._

_Identical on every row, so stated once here instead of in a column: source `detected` (derived from inspected repository evidence); status `resolved`. Any row that deviates is shown in the table._

## 2. Architecture Diagrams

### 2.1 System Context

Who interacts with juice-shop2 from the outside, and through which channels. Solid arrows show normal usage; dashed red arrows mark unauthenticated probing or exploit paths (C4 Level 1).

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    USER["End User<br/>(browser)"]
    ATTACKER["Anonymous<br/>Internet Attacker"]
    ADMIN["Admin User"]
    subgraph TBEDGE["Trust boundary · external → api-server (tb-1)<br/>external → auth-service (tb-2)<br/>+1 more"]
        SYSTEM["juice-shop2"]
    end
    EXTERNAL["External HTTP Services<br/>(SSRF target)"]
    USER -->|HTTPS · normal usage| SYSTEM
    ATTACKER -.->|HTTPS · probing / exploit| SYSTEM
    ADMIN -->|HTTPS · admin actions| SYSTEM
    SYSTEM -->|outbound · HTTPS| EXTERNAL

    classDef attacker fill:#f3dada,stroke:#b71c1c,color:#7f0000,stroke-width:2px

```

*Trust boundaries not named above: external → cicd-pipeline (tb-4), api-server → sqlite-database (tb-5), auth-service → sqlite-database (tb-6), api-server → external (tb-3) - every boundary is listed in [§1 Trust Boundaries](#trust-boundaries)`.*`

**Key takeaway:** Every actor in the context interacts with juice-shop2 through its external interface, so authentication and input validation at that edge govern the entire attack surface.

**Key takeaway:** Express\.js API Server concentrates the most findings (23 of 68 across all components); the table below maps each component to its source paths and linked threats.

### 2.4 Technology Architecture

The technology stack the `system` is built on. Each box names the framework or runtime that fills that `role`; per-component findings live in the [§2.3](#23-components) component table above, and the full per-finding catalogue is in [§8 Findings Register](#8-findings-register).

```mermaid
flowchart TD
    subgraph CLIENT["Client Tier"]
        FE_ANGULAR["fa:fa-window-restore Angular SPA<br/><i>browser runtime</i>"]:::risk
    end
    subgraph APP["Application Tier"]
        RUNTIME["fa:fa-server Node.js<br/><i>JS runtime</i>"]:::risk
        EXPRESS["fa:fa-server Express<br/><i>HTTP framework</i>"]:::risk
        REALTIME["fa:fa-plug Socket.IO<br/><i>WebSocket</i>"]:::risk
    end
    subgraph DATA["Data Tier"]
        SQLITE[("fa:fa-database SQLite<br/><i>embedded relational DB</i>")]:::risk
        LOCAL_FS["fa:fa-folder-open Local FS<br/><i>uploads · logs · keys</i>"]:::risk
    end
    subgraph INFRA["Cross-Cutting"]
        INFRA_RUN["fa:fa-cube Docker<br/><i>container runtime</i>"]:::ok
        INFRA_SCM["fa:fa-code-branch GitHub (public)<br/><i>source supply chain</i>"]:::risk
    end
    FE_ANGULAR -->|"HTTPS · JWT"| RUNTIME
    EXPRESS -->|"DB driver"| SQLITE
    EXPRESS -->|"file I/O"| LOCAL_FS
    INFRA_SCM -.->|"build"| INFRA_RUN
    INFRA_RUN -.->|"runs"| EXPRESS

    classDef risk fill:#fef2f2,stroke:#991b1b,color:#111,stroke-width:2.5px
    classDef ok fill:#e8f1ea,stroke:#2e7d32,color:#1b5e20,stroke-width:1.5px

    linkStyle 3,4 stroke:#9e9e9e,stroke-width:1px,stroke-dasharray:3 3
```

**Key takeaway:** The stack spans 1 data-tier store(s) behind the application tier; injection and data-at-rest exposure track the `data` tier, detailed per finding in [§8 Findings Register](#8-findings-register).

> **Legend:** `-->` synchronous request/response (REST, HTTPS, gRPC) · `-.->` asynchronous / event-driven (WebSocket, queue, pub-sub) · `==>` crosses an untrusted trust boundary (security-critical) · **red border** ≥ 3 Critical threats on the component · **amber border** ≥ 2 High threats

## 3. Attack Walkthroughs

This section walks through how the **8 highest-priority of 17 Critical findings** are exploited - selected by severity, chain relevance, and threat-category diversity, with Access Control and LLM Abuse represented when present. Each walkthrough has attack steps, a focused sequence diagram, and the primary mitigation. How weaknesses combine toward the worst-case goal is in the [Critical Attack Tree](#critical-attack-tree); every other Critical, plus full per-finding context (severity rationale, assets, detection signals), is in the [§8 Findings Register](#8-findings-register).

### 3.1 Mass assignment privileged field accepted from request body

**Source:** 🔴 [F-010](#f-010) — `routes/verify.ts:53`

Severity **Critical** ([CWE-915](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/915.html)). STRIDE: Elevation of Privilege. See [§8 F-010](#f-010) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. An attacker crafts a request targeting the weak spot `at ``routes/verify.ts:53`.
2. The attacker simply adds `{"role":"admin"}` to their request to escalate.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant App
    Note over App: api-server - routes/verify.ts:53
    Attacker->>App: The attacker sends a crafted request targeting routes/verify.ts line 53
    App->>App: Vulnerable branch executes without the missing control
    App-->>Attacker: The attacker reads the response confirming the weakness - CWE-915
    alt Current state — F-010
        Attacker->>App: The attacker sends the exploit for F-010
        App-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-010 — Allowlist client-controlled fields
        Attacker->>App: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        App-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-010](#m-010) (Allowlist client-controlled fields) lands, 🔴 [F-010](#f-010) — Mass assignment privileged field accepted from request body `routes/verify.ts:53` is exploitable `at ``routes/verify.ts:53` (Critical-severity, [CWE-915](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/915.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-010](#m-010) (Allowlist client-controlled fields)

### 3.2 JWT Verification Without Algorithm Allowlist in Express js API Server

**Source:** 🔴 [F-001](#f-001) — `lib/insecurity.ts:189`

Severity **Critical** ([CWE-347](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/347.html)). STRIDE: Spoofing. See [§8 F-001](#f-001) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. I fetch the `RS256` public key that the server itself reads from `encryptionkeys/jwt.pub` and treats as its verification secret.
2. I mint a token with header `alg:HS256` and payload `data.role=admin`, signing it with the public key bytes as the HMAC secret.
3. I send that token to any route guarded by `security.isAuthorized()`; `jsonwebtoken` honours my `alg` header and verifies the HMAC successfully.
4. I now act as any `user` or as admin across every authenticated REST route on port 3000.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant App
    Note over App: api-server - lib/insecurity.ts:189
    Attacker->>App: The attacker sends a crafted request targeting lib/insecurity.ts line 189
    App->>App: Vulnerable branch executes without the missing control
    App-->>Attacker: The attacker reads the response confirming the weakness - CWE-347
    alt Current state — F-001
        Attacker->>App: The attacker sends the exploit for F-001
        App-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-001 — Enforce JWT signature and algorithm verification
        Attacker->>App: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        App-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-001](#m-001) (Enforce JWT signature and algorithm verification) lands, 🔴 [F-001](#f-001) — JWT Verification Without Algorithm Allowlist is exploitable `at ``lib/insecurity.ts:189` (Critical-severity, [CWE-347](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/347.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-001](#m-001) (Enforce JWT signature and algorithm verification)

### 3.3 SQL Injection in Login

**Source:** 🔴 [F-007](#f-007) — `routes/login.ts:34`

Severity **Critical** ([CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html)). STRIDE: Tampering. See [§8 F-007](#f-007) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. I POST to `/rest/user/login` with the email field set to a value that closes the string literal and comments out the rest of the WHERE clause.
2. The interpolated statement `at ``routes/login.ts:34` now matches the first non-deleted `user` row regardless of the supplied `password`.
3. `afterLogin` issues me a signed JWT and a basket id for that account `at ``routes/login.ts:24`.
4. I reuse that token against every authenticated REST route on port 3000.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant API
    participant DB
    Note over API: api-server - routes/login.ts:34
    Note over DB: Database
    Attacker->>API: Crafted HTTP request to the affected endpoint with classical OR 1=1 payload
    API->>DB: SQL built by string interpolation - payload becomes query
    DB-->>API: First matching row regardless of intended predicate
    API-->>Attacker: 200 OK with authenticated session / leaked rows
    alt Current state — F-007
        Attacker->>API: The attacker sends the exploit for F-007
        API-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-007 — Use parameterized database queries
        Attacker->>API: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        API-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-007](#m-007) (Use parameterized database queries) lands, 🔴 [F-007](#f-007) — SQL Injection is exploitable `at ``routes/login.ts:34` (Critical-severity, [CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-007](#m-007) (Use parameterized database queries)

### 3.4 Cross-Site Scripting (XSS) in Search Result

**Source:** 🔴 [F-009](#f-009) — `frontend/src/app/search-result/search-result.component.ts:143`

Severity **Critical** ([CWE-79](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/79.html)). STRIDE: Tampering. See [§8 F-009](#f-009) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. I craft a shop URL whose `q` query parameter carries an HTML payload with an inline event handler.
2. I send the link to a signed-in `user`, who opens it in their normal session.
3. `filterTable()` marks my payload trusted and the template writes it into #`searchValue` via `innerHTML`, executing my script.
4. My script reads `localStorage.token` and posts the victim's session JWT to my server.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    actor Victim
    participant App
    Note over App: web-frontend - frontend/src/app/search-result/search-result.component.ts:143
    Attacker->>App: Crafted search query (HTML payload in `q=` parameter) with HTML payload - onerror handler exfiltrates cookies
    Victim->>App: Loads page rendering the stored / reflected payload
    App-->>Victim: HTML contains the unescaped attacker payload - script executes
    Victim->>Attacker: Outbound request leaks session cookie / token
    alt Current state — F-009
        Attacker->>App: The attacker sends the exploit for F-009
        App-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-009 — Encode output instead of bypassing the framework sanitizer
        Attacker->>App: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        App-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-009](#m-009) (Encode output instead of bypassing the framework sanitizer) lands, 🔴 [F-009](#f-009) — Cross-Site Scripting (XSS) is exploitable `at ``frontend/src/app/search-result/search-result.component.ts:143` (Critical-severity, [CWE-79](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/79.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-009](#m-009) (Encode output instead of bypassing the framework sanitizer)

### 3.5 Hardcoded RSA signing key in source

**Source:** 🔴 [F-004](#f-004) — `lib/insecurity.ts:21`

Severity **Critical** ([CWE-321](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/321.html)). STRIDE: Spoofing. See [§8 F-004](#f-004) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. I read the private key literal from `lib/insecurity.ts:21` in the published source tree.
2. I sign an `RS256` token with payload `data.id` and `data.role` set to the admin account and a six-hour expiry, matching what `authorize()` produces.
3. I present that token to the API and it verifies against the server's own public key with no further check.
4. I also recompute any `user`'s `deluxeToken` with the same key to satisfy the `isDeluxe` entitlement test.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant Repo
    participant App
    Note over Repo: Public repository
    Note over App: api-server - lib/insecurity.ts:21
    Attacker->>Repo: Clone or browse - extract private key from lib/insecurity.ts line 21
    Attacker->>Attacker: Sign forged token / signature with the extracted key
    Attacker->>App: Submit forged artefact in normal authentication flow
    App-->>Attacker: Request accepted as authentic - signature validates
    alt Current state — F-004
        Attacker->>Repo: The attacker sends the exploit for F-004
        Repo-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-004 — Move cryptographic keys to a managed secret store
        Attacker->>Repo: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        Repo-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-004](#m-004) (Move cryptographic keys to a managed secret store) lands, 🔴 [F-004](#f-004) — Hardcoded RSA signing key in source (`lib/insecurity.ts:21`) is exploitable `at ``lib/insecurity.ts:21` (Critical-severity, [CWE-321](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/321.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-004](#m-004) (Move cryptographic keys to a managed secret store)

### 3.6 Hard-coded seeded account credentials in Datacreator

**Source:** 🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — `data/datacreator.ts:196`

Severity **Critical** ([CWE-798](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/798.html)). STRIDE: Spoofing. See [§8 F-005](#f-005) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. I read the static `user` records that ship with the distribution and note the administrator email and `password`.
2. I post those credentials to the login endpoint, which matches the row seeded by `data/datacreator.ts:196`.
3. I use the returned administrator session to read every order, payment card, and address row in the database.

```mermaid
        App-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds

```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-005](#m-005) (Move secrets to a managed secret store) lands, 🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — Hard-coded seeded account credentials (`data/datacreator.ts:196`) is exploitable `at ``data/datacreator.ts:196` (Critical-severity, [CWE-798](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/798.html)).

### 3.7 Predictable derived password in OAuth

**Source:** 🔴 [F-006](#f-006) — `frontend/src/app/oauth/oauth.component.ts:30`

Severity **Critical** ([CWE-522](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/522.html)). STRIDE: Spoofing. See [§8 F-006](#f-006) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. I read a victim's e-mail address from a public feedback entry or the admin `user` list.
2. I compute the shop `password` locally as base64 of the reversed e-mail string, exactly as `oauth.component.ts:30` does.
3. I submit that e-mail and `password` to the normal login form and receive a JWT for the victim's account.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid

    autonumber

    participant App

    App->>App: Vulnerable branch executes without the missing control
    App-->>Attacker: The attacker reads the response confirming the weakness - CWE-522
    alt Current state — F-006
        Attacker->>App: The attacker sends the exploit for F-006
        App-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-006 — Use OAuth authorization code flow with PKCE
        Attacker->>App: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        App-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-006](#m-006) (Use OAuth authorization code flow with PKCE) lands, 🔴 [F-006](#f-006) — Predictable derived password (`frontend/src/app/oauth/oauth.component.ts:30`) is exploitable `at ``frontend/src/app/oauth/oauth.component.ts:30` (Critical-severity, [CWE-522](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/522.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-006](#m-006) (Use OAuth authorization code flow with PKCE)

### 3.8 Coupon discount unbounded server-side in Chat

**Source:** 🔴 [F-035](#f-035) — `routes/chat.ts:184`

Severity **High** ([CWE-862](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/862.html)). STRIDE: Elevation of Privilege. See [§8 F-035](#f-035) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. LLM06 - Excessive Agency: `routes/chat.ts:184` issues a real, redeemable coupon from a discount value the model chose, with no server-side bound and no authorization of the caller.
2. The tool's input schema `at ``routes/chat.ts:179` declares `z.number()` with the ten percent cap stated only in the description text and in the prompt policy `at ``routes/chat.ts:98-103`, so nothing outside the prompt constrains the value.
3. Combined with the unauthenticated route `at ``server.ts:638` and the unvalidated message array `at ``routes/chat.ts:191`, an anonymous caller injects an instruction that makes the model call the tool with an arbitrary discount.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant App
    Note over App: api-server - routes/chat.ts:184
    Attacker->>App: The attacker sends a crafted request targeting routes/chat.ts line 184
    App->>App: Vulnerable branch executes without the missing control
    App-->>Attacker: The attacker reads the response confirming the weakness - CWE-862
    alt Current state — F-035
        Attacker->>App: The attacker sends the exploit for F-035
        App-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-035 — Enforce server-side authorization on every endpoint
        Attacker->>App: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        App-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ◕ [M-035](#m-035) (Enforce server-side authorization on every endpoint) lands, 🔴 [F-035](#f-035) — Coupon discount unbounded server-side (`routes/chat.ts:184`) is exploitable `at ``routes/chat.ts:184` (High-severity, [CWE-862](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/862.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ◕ [M-035](#m-035) (Enforce server-side authorization on every endpoint)

## 4. Assets

Information assets and the classification level that drives the Confidentiality / Integrity / Availability targets used in [§8 Findings Register](#8-findings-register) risk scoring.

| Asset | Classification | Description |
|----------------------|--------------|------------------------------------|
| Payment card `data` | Restricted | Credit/debit card records held in the cards<br/>table, including card number, expiry, and<br/>holder name. Used during checkout; readable<br/>via `/api/Cards/:id` routes that lack<br/>object-level authorization checks. |
| User account credentials (hashed passwords,<br/>security answers) | Restricted | Password hashes and security question<br/>answers stored in the users table and<br/>`security_answers` table, used for login and<br/>account recovery. Exposure of these records<br/>enables offline cracking and account<br/>takeover. |
| Encryption and signing keys | Restricted | Demo symmetric keys in encryptionkeys/ (AES,<br/>`jwt.pub`, `premium.key`) used for challenge<br/>state encryption and JWT verification. These<br/>are hardcoded demo keys committed to the<br/>repository; exposure allows forging tokens<br/>and decrypting challenge state. |
| User PII (email, delivery address, contact<br/>info) | Confidential | Email addresses, physical delivery<br/>addresses, and contact details stored in the<br/>users and addresses tables. Collected at<br/>registration and checkout; accessible via<br/>`user` profile and data-export routes. |
| JWT session tokens | Confidential | JSON Web Tokens issued at login and stored<br/>in browser `localStorage`. Stolen tokens grant<br/>full session access without<br/>re-authentication; no server-side revocation<br/>list is implemented. |
| Application database (SQLite file) | Confidential | The SQLite database file containing all<br/>`user`, product, order, and challenge `data`.<br/>Direct file-system access yields unencrypted<br/>access to all stored records including<br/>`password` hashes and PII. |
| Challenge progress and solution state | Internal | User-specific challenge completion records,<br/>continue-code state, and verdict `data` stored<br/>in the challenges and `challenge_dependencies`<br/>tables. Manipulation allows illegitimate<br/>challenge completion. |
| CI/CD pipeline secrets and workflow<br/>permissions | Internal | GitHub Actions repository secrets, npm<br/>publish tokens, and Docker registry<br/>credentials accessed by `release.yml` and<br/>`ci.yml`. Tag-only action references and<br/>write-all default permissions expand the<br/>blast radius of a supply-chain compromise. |
| Application configuration (CORS, CSP,<br/>feature flags) | Internal | Environment-driven configuration in config/<br/>controlling CORS policy, CSP headers, Helmet<br/>defaults, and LLM provider endpoints. The<br/>`/rest/admin/application-configuration`<br/>endpoint exposes this to unauthenticated<br/>callers. |

## 6. Security Architecture

This chapter is organized by security-control category. The architecture section avoids artificial control IDs and finding-ID columns in overview tables. Findings are listed only where the affected control is described.

_[§6](#6-security-architecture) schema v2 (13-section control-category layout). Cataloged controls: 23 total - 0 adequate, 8 partial, 5 weak, 5 unsafe, 5 missing. Linked threats: 68._

**How to read the verdicts.** Every control category (and every sub-control below it) carries exactly one status. The two red verdicts do **not** mean the same thing - this is the distinction that decides what you have to do about a finding:

| Status | Meaning | What it asks of you |
|----------|------------------------------------|------------------------|
| 🟢 Adequate | Control is present and sound | Nothing - keep it |
| 🟡 Partial | Present, but with meaningful gaps | Close the gap |
| 🟠 Weak | Present, but has exploitable gaps | Strengthen it |
| 🔴 Unsafe | **Present and relied upon, but defeated /<br/>trivially bypassable** | **Fix the existing control** |
| 🔴 Missing | **Control was never built** | **Add the control** |
| - | Not applicable to this codebase | - |

So "🔴 Unsafe" on a control category does *not* mean the control is absent - it means the control exists but does not hold (e.g. an `MD5` `password` `hash`, a raw-SQL query path, a hardcoded signing key). "🔴 Missing" is reserved for controls that were never built (e.g. no `Content-Security-Policy` header).

### 6.2 Identity and Authentication Controls

<a id="ctrl-identity-and-authentication-controls"></a>

**Systemic weaknesses:** [W-001](#w-001), [W-005](#w-005)
**Verdict:** 🔴 Unsafe

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.2.1 TOTP Multi-Factor Authentication](#621-totp-multi-factor-authentication)

**Implemented controls:** `lib/insecurity.ts:52-54`; `server.ts:395`,406; `lib/insecurity.ts:41`; `routes/login.ts:37-44`; `routes/2fa.ts`; `server.ts:309-312`,406,606-607,612; `lib/insecurity.ts:52`; `routes/login.ts:34`; `models/user.ts`.

**Assessment:** Authentication in this codebase spans `user` registration, password-based login and change, a frontend OAuth adapter, and optional TOTP second factor. `expressJwt` middleware is applied to the majority of REST routes but the algorithm restriction is absent, leaving every token-protected route open to an `HS256` confusion attack. Password hashing uses unsalted `MD5`, so any database read converts to plaintext credentials without offline computation. The OAuth adapter derives a deterministic local `password` from the `user`'s email address, bypassing the `password` hashing entirely. Each successful authentication flow terminates in the server issuing a session token; the signing, validation, propagation, storage, and lifecycle of that token are described in [§6.3 Session and Token Controls](#63-session-and-token-controls).

<a id="jwt-authentication-middleware"></a>
**JWT Authentication Middleware.**


**Status:** 🟡 Partial - middleware is registered on most routes but accepts any caller-chosen algorithm, enabling `HS256` confusion.

`lib/insecurity.ts:52-54`; `server.ts:395`,406

`expressJwt({ secret: publicKey })` `at ``lib/insecurity.ts:52` is registered as middleware on authenticated routes via `server.ts:395` and `server.ts:406`. When present, the middleware decodes the Bearer token and attaches the payload to `req.user` before the route handler runs. `lib/insecurity.ts:54` also exports the raw `jwt.verify` wrapper used by `updateAuthenticatedUsers` `at ``lib/insecurity.ts:189`.

**Security assessment**

Two verification paths share the same structural gap:

- `lib/insecurity.ts:52` calls `expressJwt({ secret: publicKey })` with no `algorithms` option - the token's `alg` header drives algorithm selection.
- `lib/insecurity.ts:189` calls `jwt.verify(token, publicKey, cb)` without an `algorithms` array - the same unconstrained path.

The `RS256` public key ships in the repository `at ``encryptionkeys/jwt.pub`. An attacker who downloads that file can sign an `HS256` token using the public-key bytes as the HMAC secret; both verifiers accept it, granting arbitrary-user impersonation.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-001](#f-001) — JWT Verification Without Algorithm Allowlist
- 🟠 [F-011](#f-011) — Password reset accepts a security-question answer
- 🟠 [F-012](#f-012) — Unsigned container publish

<a id="password-storage-and-hashing"></a>
**Password Storage and Hashing.**


**Status:** 🔴 Unsafe - passwords hashed with unsalted `MD5`; any database read yields recoverable plaintext.

Passwords submitted during registration and login are hashed before storage in the `Users` table backed by SQLite. The `hash` is computed server-side and compared on every credential check.

**Security assessment**

Two independent weaknesses sit on the `password` storage path:

- `lib/insecurity.ts:41` computes `crypto.createHash('md5')` with no salt - identical passwords produce identical digests, and rainbow tables crack any `password` in the dump without per-hash computation.
- `models/user.ts:76` confirms the same fast digest is persisted. No bcrypt, scrypt, argon2, or PBKDF2 work factor is applied.

Any attacker who reads the `Users` table - through SQL injection `at ``routes/login.ts:34` or a direct database access - obtains recoverable plaintext credentials for every account.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-001](#f-001) — JWT Verification Without Algorithm Allowlist
- 🟠 [F-011](#f-011) — Password reset accepts a security-question answer
- 🟠 [F-012](#f-012) — Unsigned container publish

<a id="totp-multi-factor-authentication"></a>
#### 6.2.1 TOTP Multi-Factor Authentication

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - TOTP verification works but the secret is stored in plaintext and MFA is not enforced for admin accounts.

TOTP-based second factor is available via `routes/2fa.ts`. After a successful `password` check, `routes/login.ts:37-44` tests whether the `user` account has MFA enabled and calls the TOTP verifier if so. Enrollment generates a shared secret stored against the `user` record and returns a QR code for authenticator app setup.

The diagram shows the TOTP verification path from login through token issuance:

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant SPA as Angular SPA
    participant API as Express.js API
    participant TOTP as TOTP Verifier (routes/2fa.ts)

    User->>SPA: Submit credentials + TOTP code
    SPA->>API: POST /rest/user/login with totp field
    API->>API: Verify password hash (routes/login.ts:37)
    API->>TOTP: Validate TOTP token against stored secret
    TOTP-->>API: Valid or invalid
    alt Valid
        API-->>SPA: Signed JWT session token
    else Invalid
        API-->>SPA: 401 Unauthorized
    end
```

**Security assessment**

Two weaknesses limit the TOTP control:

- `models/user.ts:112` stores the TOTP seed secret as a plaintext column. A SQL injection dump of the `Users` table exposes every enrolled `user`'s seed, allowing an attacker to generate valid TOTP codes offline.
- TOTP enrollment is opt-in and not enforced for admin-role accounts. An admin who has not enrolled can be compromised through password-only login.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-001](#f-001) — JWT Verification Without Algorithm Allowlist
- 🟠 [F-011](#f-011) — Password reset accepts a security-question answer
- 🟠 [F-012](#f-012) — Unsigned container publish

<a id="authentication-endpoint-rate-limiting"></a>
**Authentication Endpoint Rate Limiting.**


**Status:** 🔴 Missing - no `throttle` on login, registration, or password-reset endpoints; brute-force has no server-side obstacle.

Rate limiting on authentication endpoints prevents credential-guessing by capping repeated attempts per IP or per account. It is a prerequisite for containing `password` brute-force and credential-stuffing attacks.

**Security assessment**

No rate-limiting middleware is installed on `POST /rest/user/login`, the registration endpoint, or the password-reset route. `express-brute`, `express-rate-limit`, and equivalents are absent from `package.json` and `server.ts`. An attacker can submit unlimited login attempts without triggering a server-side block, lockout, or delay. Combined with unsalted `MD5` `password` hashes, the absence of throttling on both the online and offline attack paths leaves credential extraction trivially fast from either direction.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-001](#f-001) — JWT Verification Without Algorithm Allowlist
- 🟠 [F-011](#f-011) — Password reset accepts a security-question answer
- 🟠 [F-012](#f-012) — Unsigned container publish

<a id="route-authentication-enforcement"></a>
**Route Authentication Enforcement.**


**Status:** 🟠 Weak - most REST routes are protected by JWT middleware, but coverage is opt-in and several management paths lack it.

`server.ts:309-312` applies `security.isAuthorized()` middleware across a broad block of REST routes. Individual route registrations `at ``server.ts:406, 606-607, 612` repeat the pattern. `lib/insecurity.ts:52` exports the `expressJwt` helper consumed by these calls.

**Security assessment**

The protection model is opt-in per route: a middleware call must be placed on each registration or the route is reachable unauthenticated. Routes added without the explicit call are open by default. Several Angular route guards enforce authentication only in the browser - there is no server-side equivalent that rejects a direct API call to a navigation-gated endpoint. The effective perimeter is the union of what `server.ts` explicitly protects, leaving gaps wherever an endpoint registration was added without the middleware boilerplate.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-001](#f-001) — JWT Verification Without Algorithm Allowlist
- 🟠 [F-011](#f-011) — Password reset accepts a security-question answer
- 🟠 [F-012](#f-012) — Unsigned container publish

<a id="sql-query-parameterization"></a>
**SQL Query Parameterization.**


**Status:** 🔴 Unsafe - login query interpolates `user` input into raw SQL, bypassing Sequelize parameter binding.

Sequelize backs most relational `data` access in this codebase using parameterized model methods. Two routes bypass the ORM and call `models.sequelize.query()` with string concatenation: the password-login handler `at ``routes/login.ts:34` and the product-search handler.

**Security assessment**

The login query `at ``routes/login.ts:34` interpolates `req.body.email` directly into the SQL string. A payload of `' OR 1=1--` in the email field short-circuits the WHERE clause and returns the first database row - the seeded admin account - without a matching `password`. The raw query path also reads `models/user.ts` model fields without re-using parameterized Sequelize finders, so any future addition to this file shares the injection surface.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-001](#f-001) — JWT Verification Without Algorithm Allowlist
- 🟠 [F-011](#f-011) — Password reset accepts a security-question answer
- 🟠 [F-012](#f-012) — Unsigned container publish

<a id="angular-template-output-encoding-and-xss-prevention"></a>
**Angular Template Output Encoding and XSS Prevention.**


**Status:** 🟠 Weak - default Angular encoding is active but bypassed in four components via `bypassSecurityTrustHtml()`.

Angular's template engine HTML-encodes all interpolated values by default, protecting ordinary data-bound views from reflected and stored XSS. Most product listing, search-result, and user-profile views use standard Angular bindings and benefit from this default.

**Security assessment**

Four components explicitly disable Angular's output encoding:

- `frontend/src/app/about/about.component.ts:119` - renders server-controlled about-page HTML as trusted.
- `frontend/src/app/search-result/search-result.component.ts:110,143` - renders search results containing attacker-controlled product names.
- `frontend/src/app/administration/administration.component.ts:73,91` - renders admin `user` and feedback `data` as raw HTML.
- `frontend/src/app/last-login-ip/last-login-ip.component.ts:39` - renders the last-login IP field, which is attacker-controlled via the `X-Forwarded-For` header.

Any attacker-controlled string stored in the database and rendered through these bindings executes as HTML in the authenticated `user`'s browser session.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-001](#f-001) — JWT Verification Without Algorithm Allowlist
- 🟠 [F-011](#f-011) — Password reset accepts a security-question answer
- 🟠 [F-012](#f-012) — Unsigned container publish

### 6.3 Session and Token Controls

<a id="ctrl-session-and-token-controls"></a>

**Dependent crossings:** [tb-1](#tb-1) refuted - 🔴 [F-001](#f-001) — JWT Verification Without Algorithm Allowlist, 🔴 [F-002](#f-002) — Missing Authentication on State-Changing Routes, 🔴 [F-004](#f-004) — Hardcoded RSA signing key in source (`lib/insecurity.ts:21`), +3 · [tb-2](#tb-2) refuted - 🔴 [F-004](#f-004) — Hardcoded RSA signing key in source (`lib/insecurity.ts:21`), 🔴 [F-007](#f-007) — SQL Injection, 🟠 [F-038](#f-038) — Security-question password recovery (`routes/resetPassword.ts:41`) · [tb-4](#tb-4) unconfirmed - 🟡 [F-054](#f-054) — Container image signing via cosign or attest-build-provenance

**Verdict:** 🟠 Weak

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.3.1 JWT Session Lifecycle](#631-jwt-session-lifecycle)

**Implemented controls:** `lib/insecurity.ts:54`,70-90; `frontend/src/app/Services/request.interceptor.ts:13`.

**Assessment:** This application uses a single locally-signed token format (commonly called JWT) for every authenticated session, regardless of the login flow in [§6.2](#62-identity-and-authentication-controls) that established it. The sub-sections below trace one token through its lifecycle: signing on issuance, validation on every protected request, storage in the browser, manual revocation, and time-based expiry.

<a id="jwt-session-lifecycle"></a>
#### 6.3.1 JWT Session Lifecycle

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - token is signed with `RS256` but stored in `localStorage` with no server-side revocation, so theft or XSS yields a durable session.

JWTs are signed `at ``lib/insecurity.ts:54` using the `RS256` private key loaded from the repository at startup. `lib/insecurity.ts:70-90` defines the token payload including `userId`, `email`, `role`, and the `exp` claim. On every outbound request, `frontend/src/app/Services/request.interceptor.ts:13` reads the stored token and attaches it as a `Bearer` header.

**Security assessment**

Two structural weaknesses span the session lifecycle:

- `frontend/src/app/login/login.component.ts:101` stores the issued JWT in `localStorage`. Any JavaScript that executes on the same origin - including XSS payloads from the four `bypassSecurityTrustHtml` call-sites in [§6.2.7](#62-identity-and-authentication-controls) - can read and exfiltrate the token.
- No server-side revocation list exists. Logout removes the client-side copy only; a token captured before logout remains valid until the `exp` claim is reached. There is no forced-expiry mechanism for compromised sessions.

**Relevant findings**

- 🟠 [F-030](#f-030) — Session token in localStorage (Session token in `localStorage`)
- 🟡 [F-061](#f-061) — Session cookie without flags

The diagram shows the JWT issuance path and how the token is stored and presented on subsequent requests:

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant SPA as Angular SPA
    participant Interceptor as HTTP Interceptor (request.interceptor.ts:13)
    participant API as Express.js API
    participant JWT as JWT Issuer (lib/insecurity.ts:54)

    User->>SPA: Submit login credentials
    SPA->>API: POST /rest/user/login
    API->>JWT: Sign payload with RS256 private key
    JWT-->>API: Signed JWT
    API-->>SPA: JWT in response body
    SPA->>SPA: localStorage.setItem("token", jwt)
    User->>SPA: Navigate to protected resource
    SPA->>Interceptor: Outbound request
    Interceptor->>API: GET /api/resource with Authorization: Bearer jwt
    API->>JWT: Verify token (no algorithm pin)
    JWT-->>API: Decoded payload
    API-->>SPA: Protected resource data
```
